It’s like I’m still switched on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others when they were speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe once in a while if I’m excited about something, but I haven’t really noticed myself cutting people off a lot.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. At work, I’ll be at the bench and start with a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then hear something outside, and before I know it I’ve lost track of the whole work order. It’s starting to mess with my day.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focusing or staying on task outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. I’ll start doing one thing and then something small catches my attention, and I’m off on that instead. Then I realize I didn’t finish what I was originally doing. It’s like my mind won’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I can’t really sit still at the bench. I’ll start sketching a new watch case, then jump to emailing suppliers, then I’m up rearranging the display trays—even after closing, I’m still moving around doing something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do jump between things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it acting without thinking. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something to someone without thinking, and then regretted it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, everybody says things they wish they hadn’t once in a while, but nothing really comes to mind as a pattern for me. I haven’t noticed that being a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not technically working, I feel like I need to be doing something. At the shop I can’t just sit at the bench—I’ll start on a watch case sketch, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m up moving display trays around, even after closing. It’s like I’m still switched on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others when they were speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe once in a while if I’m excited about something, but I haven’t really noticed myself cutting people off a lot.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wasn’t told I was unusually restless or anything compared to other kids. I was active, I guess, but I don’t remember it being something people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, lately I have. My shop records got really disorganized, and I missed a major custom delivery because I couldn’t keep everything straight. It got bad enough that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family’s been checking on me more too, because I’ve kind of slipped out of my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or having a lot of energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember. I don’t think people called me fidgety or said I had too much energy as a kid. At least, nobody in my family made a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t really remember having these problems as a kid. It feels more like something I’ve noticed more recently.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure of an exact age. I don’t remember it being there when I was younger. I guess I only started noticing it more as an adult, but I can’t pin down when it started.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having problems like that in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I wasn’t known for not paying attention or not being able to sit still, at least not that I can recall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t keep my mind on one thing lately. At work, I’ll be at the bench and start with a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then hear something outside, and before I know it I’ve lost track of the whole work order. It’s starting to mess with my day.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focusing or staying on task outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. I’ll start doing one thing and then something small catches my attention, and I’m off on that instead. Then I realize I didn’t finish what I was originally doing. It’s like my mind won’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I can’t really sit still at the bench. I’ll start sketching a new watch case, then jump to emailing suppliers, then I’m up rearranging the display trays—even after closing, I’m still moving around doing something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do jump between things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it acting without thinking. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something to someone without thinking, and then regretted it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, everybody says things they wish they hadn’t once in a while, but nothing really comes to mind as a pattern for me. I haven’t noticed that being a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not technically working, I feel like I need to be doing something. At the shop I can’t just sit at the bench—I’ll start on a watch case sketch, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m up moving display trays around, even after closing. It’s like I’m still switched on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others when they were speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe once in a while if I’m excited about something, but I haven’t really noticed myself cutting people off a lot.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wasn’t told I was unusually restless or anything compared to other kids. I was active, I guess, but I don’t remember it being something people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, lately I have. My shop records got really disorganized, and I missed a major custom delivery because I couldn’t keep everything straight. It got bad enough that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family’s been checking on me more too, because I’ve kind of slipped out of my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or having a lot of energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember. I don’t think people called me fidgety or said I had too much energy as a kid. At least, nobody in my family made a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t really remember having these problems as a kid. It feels more like something I’ve noticed more recently.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure of an exact age. I don’t remember it being there when I was younger. I guess I only started noticing it more as an adult, but I can’t pin down when it started.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having problems like that in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I wasn’t known for not paying attention or not being able to sit still, at least not that I can recall.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your behavior or attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember. I don’t think my parents or teachers ever sat me down or said they were worried about my attention or behavior. At least, nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something impulsively that had significant negative consequences for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I can’t think of a specific time where I did something impulsively and it caused major consequences. I’ve made some bad choices like anyone, but nothing that really stands out to me that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll start sketching a new watch case, then jump to emailing suppliers, then I’m up rearranging the display trays—even after closing, I’m still moving around doing something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do jump between things a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it acting without thinking. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something to someone without thinking, and then regretted it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, everybody says things they wish they hadn’t once in a while, but nothing really comes to mind as a pattern for me. I haven’t noticed that being a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not technically working, I feel like I need to be doing something. At the shop I can’t just sit at the bench—I’ll start on a watch case sketch, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m up moving display trays around, even after closing. It’s like I’m still switched on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others when they were speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe once in a while if I’m excited about something, but I haven’t really noticed myself cutting people off a lot.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wasn’t told I was unusually restless or anything compared to other kids. I was active, I guess, but I don’t remember it being something people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, lately I have. My shop records got really disorganized, and I missed a major custom delivery because I couldn’t keep everything straight. It got bad enough that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family’s been checking on me more too, because I’ve kind of slipped out of my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or having a lot of energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember. I don’t think people called me fidgety or said I had too much energy as a kid. At least, nobody in my family made a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been as long as I can remember. I don’t really remember having these problems as a kid. It feels more like something I’ve noticed more recently.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure of an exact age. I don’t remember it being there when I was younger. I guess I only started noticing it more as an adult, but I can’t pin down when it started.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having problems like that in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I wasn’t known for not paying attention or not being able to sit still, at least not that I can recall.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your behavior or attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember. I don’t think my parents or teachers ever sat me down or said they were worried about my attention or behavior. At least, nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something impulsively that had significant negative consequences for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I can’t think of a specific time where I did something impulsively and it caused major consequences. I’ve made some bad choices like anyone, but nothing that really stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever considered impulsive as a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I don’t remember people describing me that way as a kid. I might’ve acted without thinking sometimes, like any kid, but I wasn’t labeled impulsive or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and restlessness significantly interfered with your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I missed a big custom delivery at work, and my shop records got so messy that I had to stop taking new jobs for now. My family’s been checking in on me more too, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off from my normal routines.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate approximately how old you were when you first started noticing these problems with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember a clear age when it started. It feels like I only noticed it more recently when it began getting in the way, but I couldn’t say when it first began.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
